Standard notation:
Vertices: Capital letters (A, B, C)
Sides: Lowercase letters (a, b, c)
Side a is opposite angle A
Side b is opposite angle B
Side c is opposite angle C
Angles at vertices: ∠A, ∠B, ∠C (or α, β, γ)

The Sine Rule
Statement: In any triangle ABC, the ratio of any side to the sine of its opposite angle is constant.

Formula: �
sin�
=    � sin�
=    � sin�
Alternative form: sin� = sin� = sin�
�	�	�
Diagram 59: Sine Rule Illustration

B
/|\
/ | \
/ | \
c /	|	\ a
/	|	\
/ α	| β \
/	|	\
A		γ	C b

Sine Rule:
a/sin α = b/sin β = c/sin γ

Or: a/sin A = b/sin B = c/sin C

When to Use Sine Rule:
Given two angles and one side (AAS or ASA)
Given two sides and a non-included angle (SSA) - ambiguous case
To find an angle when two sides and one opposite angle are known
Types of Problems:
Type 1: Finding a side (given two angles and one side)
Type 2: Finding an angle (given two sides and one opposite angle)
The Cosine Rule
Statement: In any triangle, the square of any side equals the sum of squares of the other two sides minus twice their product times the cosine of the included angle.
Formulas: �² = �² + �² − 2��cos� �² = �² + �² − 2��cos� �² = �² + �² − 2��cos�

For finding angles: cos� = �²+�²−�² cos� = �²+�²−�² cos� = �²+�²−�²
2��
2��
2��
Diagram 60: Cosine Rule Illustration
B
/|\
/ | \
/ | \
c /	|	\ a
/	|	\
/ A	|	\
/	|	\ A			C
b

Cosine Rule:
a² = b² + c² - 2bc cos A b² = a² + c² - 2ac cos B c² = a² + b² - 2ab cos C
When to Use Cosine Rule:
Given three sides (SSS) - to find any angle
Given two sides and the included angle (SAS) - to find the third side
When sine rule cannot be applied
Special Case: When angle = 90°, cosine rule reduces to Pythagoras' theorem (since cos 90° = 0)
Choosing Between Sine and Cosine Rules

Decision Chart:
Diagram 61: Rule Selection Guide
Given Information	Use This Rule
──────────────────────────────────────
2 angles + 1 side	→ Sine Rule (AAS or ASA)

sides + included angle → Cosine Rule (SAS)

sides	→ Cosine Rule
(SSS)	(to find angles)

2 sides + opposite angle → Sine Rule
(SSA)	(check for ambiguous case)

Problem-Solving Steps:
Draw and label the triangle
Identify given information
Determine what to find
Choose appropriate rule
Substitute values carefully
Calculate using calculator
Check answer is reasonable
Worked Examples:
Example 1: In triangle ABC, ∠A = 40°, ∠B = 65°, and side a = 8 cm. Find side b.
Diagram 62:
B
/|\
/ | \
/ | \
c /	|	\ a=8cm
/	|	\
/65° | 40°\
/	|	\ A			C
b

Solution: Using sine rule: a/sin A = b/sin B 8/sin 40° = b/sin 65°
b = (8 × sin 65°)/sin 40° b = (8 × 0.9063)/0.6428 b = 7.251/0.6428 b ≈ 11.28 cm
Example 2: In triangle PQR, p = 7 cm, q = 9 cm, and ∠R = 58°. Find side r.

Diagram 63:
Q

/ | \
r ? /	|	\ p=7cm
/	|	\
/	|58° \
/	|	\ P			R
q=9cm

Solution: Using cosine rule: r² = p² + q² - 2pq cos R
r² = 7² + 9² - 2(7)(9)cos 58° r² = 49 + 81 - 126 × 0.5299 r² = 130 - 66.77 r² = 63.23 r = √63.23 r ≈
7.95 cm
Example 3: In triangle ABC, a = 12 cm, b = 15 cm, and c = 18 cm. Find angle A.
Diagram 64:
B
/|\
/ | \
/ | \
c=18 /	|	\ a=12
/	|	\
/ A? |	\
/	|	\ A			C
b=15

Solution: Using cosine rule: cos A = (b² + c² - a²)/(2bc)
cos A = (15² + 18² - 12²)/(2 × 15 × 18) cos A = (225 + 324 - 144)/540 cos A = 405/540 cos A = 0.75
A = cos⁻¹(0.75) A ≈ 41.41°
Example 4: In triangle XYZ, ∠X = 35°, ∠Y = 75°, and z = 20 cm. Find: (a) ∠Z (b) Side x (c) Side y

Diagram 65:
Y
/|\
/ | \
/ | \ z=20/	|	\ x=?
/	|	\
/ 75° | 35°\
/	|	\ X			Z
y=?

Solution: (a) ∠Z = 180° - 35° - 75° = 70° (sum of angles in triangle)
Using sine rule: x/sin X = z/sin Z x/sin 35° = 20/sin 70° x = (20 × sin 35°)/sin 70° x = (20 × 0.5736)/0.9397 x ≈ 12.21 cm
Using sine rule: y/sin Y = z/sin Z y/sin 75° = 20/sin 70° y = (20 × sin 75°)/sin 70° y = (20 × 0.9659)/0.9397 y ≈ 20.56 cm
Example 5: Two sides of a triangle are 8 cm and 11 cm, and the included angle is 64°. Find the third side.
Diagram 66:
B

/	|	\
/ 64° |	\
/	|	\ A			C
b=8

Solution: Using cosine rule: a² = b² + c² - 2bc cos A
a² = 8² + 11² - 2(8)(11)cos 64° a² = 64 + 121 - 176 × 0.4384 a² = 185 - 77.16 a² = 107.84 a =
√107.84 a ≈ 10.38 cm
Example 6: In triangle ABC, a = 10 cm, b = 14 cm, and ∠A = 38°. Find ∠B.

Diagram 67:
B
/|\
/ | \
/ | \
c=? /	|	\ a=10
/	|	\
/ B? | 38°\
/	|	\ A			C
b=14

Solution: Using sine rule: a/sin A = b/sin B 10/sin 38° = 14/sin B
sin B = (14 × sin 38°)/10 sin B = (14 × 0.6157)/10 sin B = 0.862
B = sin⁻¹(0.862) B ≈ 59.57° or B ≈ 120.43° (two possible solutions - ambiguous case)
Since b > a, the larger angle is opposite the larger side, so B must be acute. Therefore, B ≈ 59.57°
Example 7: A triangle has sides of length 5 cm, 7 cm, and 9 cm. Find the largest angle.

Solution: The largest angle is opposite the longest side. Longest side is b = 9 cm, so find angle B.
Using cosine rule: cos B = (a² + c² - b²)/(2ac)
cos B = (5² + 7² - 9²)/(2 × 5 × 7) cos B = (25 + 49 - 81)/70 cos B = -7/70 cos B = -0.1 B = cos⁻¹(-0.1) B ≈ 95.74°
Example 8 (Application): Two ships leave a port at the same time. One travels at 30 km/h on a bearing of 040° and the other at 25 km/h on a bearing of 130°. How far apart are they after 2 hours?
Diagram 69:

|\ 130°

| \Ship 2
|	\

Solution: Distance traveled by Ship 1 = 30 × 2 = 60 km Distance traveled by Ship 2 = 25 × 2 =
50 km
Angle between paths = 130° - 40° = 90°
Using cosine rule (or Pythagoras since angle = 90°): d² = 60² + 50² - 2(60)(50)cos 90° d² = 3600
+ 2500 - 0 (since cos 90° = 0) d² = 6100 d = √6100 d ≈ 78.10 km The ships are approximately 78.10 km apart.

WEEK 8: TRIGONOMETRY II
Learning Objectives:
By the end of the lesson, students should be able to:
State and apply Pythagoras' theorem
Solve 2D problems involving right-angled triangles
Understand and calculate angles of elevation and depression
Apply trigonometry to real-life problems
Use trigonometric ratios (sin, cos, tan) effectively
Entry Behaviour:
Students understand basic trigonometric ratios and can identify right-angled triangles.
Instructional Materials:
Calculator (scientific)
Clinometer (angle measuring device)
Protractor and ruler
Right-angled triangle models
Pictures showing elevation/depression
Whiteboard and markers
Content:
Pythagoras' Theorem
Statement: In a right-angled triangle, the square of the hypotenuse equals the sum of squares of the other two sides.
Formula: c² = a² + b² Where:
c = hypotenuse (longest side, opposite the right angle)
a and b = the other two sides (legs)
Diagram 70: Pythagoras' Theorem
|\
| \
| \ c (hypotenuse) a |	\
|	\
|		\ b

c² = a² + b²

Finding different sides:
Hypotenuse: c = √(a² + b²)
- Leg: a = √(c² - b²)
- Leg: b = √(c² - a²)

Visual Proof:
Diagram 71: Area Demonstration
c²	a²	b²

||||||||||||||=|	|+|||||||	|	|	|

Area of square on hypotenuse =
Sum of areas of squares on other two sides

Pythagorean Triples: Common sets of integers that satisfy Pythagoras' theorem:
(3, 4, 5)
(5, 12, 13)
(8, 15, 17)
(7, 24, 25)
Multiples of these also work: (6, 8, 10), (9, 12, 15), etc.
Trigonometric Ratios Review
Diagram 72: SOHCAHTOA
|\
| \
Opposite	| \ Hypotenuse (Opp)	|	\
|θ	\ Adjacent (Adj)

sin θ = Opposite/Hypotenuse = Opp/Hyp cos θ = Adjacent/Hypotenuse = Adj/Hyp tan θ = Opposite/Adjacent = Opp/Adj

Memory aid: SOHCAHTOA
SOH: Sin = Opposite/Hypotenuse
CAH: Cos = Adjacent/Hypotenuse
TOA: Tan = Opposite/Adjacent

Special Angles:
Diagram 73: Special Angle Values
Angle	sin	cos	tan
──────────────────────────────

0°01030°1/2√3/21/√345°1/√21/√2160°√3/21/2√390°10undefined
These should be memorized or referenced

Angles of Elevation and Depression
Angle of Elevation: The angle between the horizontal line and the line of sight when looking UP at an object.
Angle of Depression: The angle between the horizontal line and the line of sight when looking DOWN at an object.

Problem-Solving in 2D
Types of Problems:
Finding heights (buildings, trees, towers)
Finding distances (horizontal or slant)
Navigation and bearings
Ladder problems
Shadow problems
Problem-Solving Steps:
Draw a clear diagram
Label all known information
Identify the right-angled triangle
Choose appropriate ratio or theorem
Set up equation
Solve for unknown
Check answer is reasonable
Worked Examples:
Example 1: A ladder 10 m long leans against a wall. If the foot of the ladder is 6 m from the wall, how high up the wall does the ladder reach?
Diagram 76:
Wall
|\
| \
| \ 10m (ladder)
h	|	\
|	\
|		\ 6m

Using Pythagoras: h² + 6² = 10²

Solution: Using Pythagoras' theorem: h² + 6² = 10² h² + 36 = 100 h² = 64 h = 8 m The ladder reaches 8 m up the wall.
Example 2: From a point 50 m from the base of a tower, the angle of elevation to the top is 28°. Find the height of the tower.
Diagram 77:
Tower
|
|
h |
|
| Observer |	|
28°
· 50m →

Solution: In the right triangle: tan 28° = h/50 h = 50 × tan 28° h = 50 × 0.5317 h ≈ 26.59 m
The tower is approximately 26.59 m tall.
Example 3: A plane flying at an altitude of 3000 m observes a point on the ground at an angle of depression of 35°. How far is the plane from that point (slant distance)?
Diagram 78:
Plane •
 	/|
35° / | 3000m
/ |
/	|
/	|
/35° |
/	|
Point	Horizontal

(Note: Angle of depression = alternate angle)

Solution: In the right triangle: sin 35° = 3000/d (where d is slant distance) d = 3000/sin 35° d = 3000/0.5736 d ≈ 5230 m
The slant distance is approximately 5230 m or 5.23 km.
Example 4: A right-angled triangle has one angle of 40° and the hypotenuse is 15 cm. Find the lengths of the other two sides.
Diagram 79:
|\
| \
| \ 15cm a |	\
|40°_\ b
Solution: For side a (opposite to 40°): sin 40° = a/15 a = 15 × sin 40° a = 15 × 0.6428 a ≈ 9.64 cm
For side b (adjacent to 40°): cos 40° = b/15 b = 15 × cos 40° b = 15 × 0.7660 b ≈ 11.49 cm Verification using Pythagoras: 9.64² + 11.49² = 92.93 + 132.02 = 224.95 ≈ 225 = 15² ✓
Example 5: A man standing on top of a 60 m cliff observes a boat at an angle of depression of
25°. How far is the boat from the base of the cliff?
Diagram 80:
Cliff top •
 	/|
25°	/ | 60m
/ |
/	|
/25° |
/	| Boat	Base
· d →

Solution: Using alternate angles, angle at base = 25° tan 25° = 60/d
d = 60/tan 25° d = 60/0.4663 d ≈ 128.65 m
The boat is approximately 128.65 m from the base of the cliff.
Example 6: A kite string is 80 m long and makes an angle of 52° with the horizontal ground. Find: (a) The height of the kite (b) The horizontal distance from the person to the point directly below the kite
Diagram 81:

Solution: (a) Height of kite: sin 52° = h/80 h = 80 × sin 52° h = 80 × 0.7880 h ≈ 63.04 m
(b) Horizontal distance: cos 52° = d/80 d = 80 × cos 52° d = 80 × 0.6157 d ≈ 49.26 m
Example 7: Two buildings are 40 m apart. From the top of the shorter building (15 m high), the angle of elevation to the top of the taller building is 32°. Find the height of the taller building.
Diagram 82:

Total height of tall building = 15 + x

Solution: tan 32° = x/40
x = 40 × tan 32° x = 40 × 0.6249 x ≈ 25.00 m
Total height of taller building = 15 + 25 = 40 m
Example 8: A rectangle has length 12 cm and width 5 cm. Find: (a) The length of the diagonal
The angle the diagonal makes with the length

Solution: (a) Using Pythagoras: d² = 12² + 5² d² = 144 + 25 d² = 169 d = 13 cm (b) tan θ = 5/12 θ = tan⁻¹(5/12) θ = tan⁻¹(0.4167) θ ≈ 22.62°
Example 9: A ship sails 15 km east, then 8 km north. Find: (a) How far the ship is from its starting point (b) The bearing of the ship from its starting point

Solution: (a) Distance from start: d² = 15² + 8² d² = 225 + 64 d² = 289 d = 17 km (b) tan θ = 8/15 θ = tan⁻¹(8/15) θ ≈ 28.07°
Bearing = 090° - 28.07° = 061.93° ≈ 062°
Or bearing = N 28° E (approximately)

Activity 2: Create a right-angled triangle with sides 6 cm, 8 cm, and 10 cm. Verify Pythagoras' theorem.

∠ACB = 90° and ∠CAB = 35°. Find the width of the river (distance AB).

WEEK 9: FRACTIONS
Learning Objectives:
By the end of the lesson, students should be able to:
Perform operations with fractions (addition, subtraction, multiplication, division)
Solve equations involving fractions
Simplify complex fractions
Apply fractions to real-life problems
Convert between mixed numbers and improper fractions
Entry Behaviour:
Students understand basic concepts of fractions, numerators, denominators, and can find LCM and HCF.
Instructional Materials:
Fraction charts and models
Whiteboard and markers
Calculator
Real-life objects for demonstration (pieces of fruit, paper, etc.)
Fraction strips or circles
Visual aids showing equivalent fractions
Content:
Review of Fraction Basics
Definition: A fraction represents a part of a whole or a division of quantities.
Diagram 85: Fraction Representation
Fraction: numerator/denominator Example: 3/4
Visual representation:
┌───┬───┬───┬───┐
│ █ │ █ │ █ │	│ 3 out of 4 parts shaded
└───┴───┴───┴───┘
3 = numerator (number of parts taken)
4 = denominator (total number of equal parts)

Types of Fractions:
Diagram 86: Types of Fractions
PROPER FRACTION (numerator < denominator) Examples: 2/5, 3/8, 7/10
│ █ │	│
│	│	│ = 2/5 (less than 1 whole)

IMPROPER FRACTION (numerator ≥ denominator) Examples: 7/4, 11/8, 5/5
│ █ │ █ │ █ │ = 7/4 (more than 1 whole)

MIXED NUMBER (whole number + proper fraction) Examples: 2¾, 3½, 5⅖
│ █ │ █ │ ¾ │ = 2¾

UNIT FRACTION (numerator = 1) Examples: 1/2, 1/5, 1/10
│ █ │ = 1/2

EQUIVALENT FRACTIONS (same value, different form) Examples: 1/2 = 2/4 = 3/6 = 4/8
│ █ │	│ = │ █	█ │	│
│		│	│	│	│	│	│	│ 1/2			2/4
Converting Between Forms: Diagram 87: Conversion Methods IMPROPER to MIXED:
Divide numerator by denominator

Example: 17/5
17 ÷ 5 = 3 remainder 2 17/5 = 3²/₅

MIXED to IMPROPER:
(Whole × Denominator) + Numerator Denominator

Example: 3²/₅

(3 × 5) + 2=1755
Addition and Subtraction of Fractions
Rule: Fractions can only be added or subtracted if they have the same denominator (common denominator).
With Same Denominators: Diagram 88: Same Denominator
Addition: a/c + b/c = (a+b)/c Example: 2/7 + 3/7 = 5/7
Visual:│ █ █ ││+│█ █ █ ││=│█ █ █ █ █ │││	│	│	││	│	│	││	│	│	│2/73/75/7
Subtraction: a/c - b/c = (a-b)/c

Example: 5/8 - 2/8 = 3/8

With Different Denominators:
Steps:
Find the Least Common Multiple (LCM) of denominators
Convert fractions to equivalent fractions with LCM as denominator
Add or subtract numerators
Simplify if possible
Diagram 89: Different Denominators Process
Example: 1/3 + 1/4

Step 1: Find LCM of 3 and 4
LCM = 12

Step 2: Convert to equivalent fractions 1/3 = 4/12 (multiply by 4/4) 1/4 = 3/12 (multiply by 3/3)

Step 3: Add
4/12 + 3/12 = 7/12

│ █ █ │ +	│ █ █ █ │ =	│ █ █ █ █ █ │

│	│	│	│	│	│

2/7	3/7	5/7

Mixed Numbers:

Diagram 90: Adding Mixed Numbers
Method 1: Convert to improper fractions Example: 2¼ + 1½
= 9/4 + 3/2
= 9/4 + 6/4
= 15/4 = 3¾

Method 2: Add whole numbers and fractions separately Example: 2¼ + 1½
= (2 + 1) + (¼ + ½)
= 3 + (¼ + 2/4)
= 3 + ¾ = 3¾

Multiplication of Fractions
Rule: Multiply numerators together and denominators together.
Formula: a/b × c/d = (a×c)/(b×d) Diagram 91: Multiplying Fractions Example: 2/3 × 3/4
Method: Numerator × Numerator Denominator × Denominator
2/3 × 3/4 = (2×3)/(3×4) = 6/12 = 1/2

Visual concept: 2/3 of 3/4
Take 3/4, then take 2/3 of that amount

┌───┬───┬───┬───┐
│ █ │ █ │ █ │	│ This is 3/4
└───┴───┴───┴───┘
↓
Take 2/3 of this
│ █ │ █ │	│	│ = 2/4 = 1/2
└───┴───┴───┴───┘

SIMPLIFICATION TIP:
Cancel common factors before multiplying

2/3 × 3/4 = 2/3̶× 3̶/4 = 2/4 = 1/2 (cancel 3s)
Multiplying Mixed Numbers:
Diagram 92: Mixed Number Multiplication
Convert to improper fractions first

Example: 2½ × 1⅓
= 5/2 × 4/3
= (5×4)/(2×3)
= 20/6
= 10/3 = 3⅓

Multiplying by Whole Numbers:
Whole number = whole number/1

Example: 3 × 2/5 = 3/1 × 2/5 = 6/5 = 1⅕

Division of Fractions
Rule: Multiply by the reciprocal (flip the second fraction).
Formula: a/b ÷ c/d = a/b × d/c
Diagram 93: Dividing Fractions
"Keep, Change, Flip" (KCF method) Example: 2/3 ÷ 4/5
Step 1: KEEP the first fraction: 2/3
Step 2: CHANGE division to multiplication: × Step 3: FLIP the second fraction: 5/4

2/3 ÷ 4/5 = 2/3 × 5/4 = 10/12 = 5/6

WHY IT WORKS:
Division asks: "How many 4/5s are in 2/3?" Multiplying by reciprocal gives the answer.
Dividing Mixed Numbers:
Diagram 94: Mixed Number Division
Convert to improper fractions first

Example: 3½ ÷ 1¼
= 7/2 ÷ 5/4
= 7/2 × 4/5
= 28/10
= 14/5 = 2⅘

Solving Equations Involving Fractions Type 1: Simple Fractional Equations Diagram 95: Basic Fractional Equations Example: x/4 = 3
Multiply both sides by 4: 4 × x/4 = 3 × 4
x = 12

Example: 2x/5 = 8

Multiply both sides by 5: 2x = 40
x = 20
Type 2: Equations with Multiple Fractions
Method: Multiply by LCM of denominators to clear fractions
Diagram 96: Multiple Fraction Equations
Example: x/2 + x/3 = 10

Step 1: Find LCM of 2 and 3 = 6

Step 2: Multiply entire equation by 6 6(x/2) + 6(x/3) = 6(10)
3x + 2x = 60

Step 3: Solve
5x = 60
x = 12

Verification: 12/2 + 12/3 = 6 + 4 = 10 ✓

Type 3: Complex Fractional Equations
Example: (x+2)/3 - (x-1)/4 = 2 LCM of 3 and 4 = 12
Multiply by 12:
12(x+2)/3 - 12(x-1)/4 = 12(2)
4(x+2) - 3(x-1) = 24
4x + 8 - 3x + 3 = 24
x + 11 = 24
x = 13

Application of Fractions to Daily Life
Common Applications:
Diagram 97: Real-Life Fraction Uses
COOKING/RECIPES
"Add ½ cup of sugar" "Use ¾ teaspoon of salt"

MEASUREMENTS
"The board is 5¾ inches wide" "Cut it into ⅓ parts"

MONEY/SHOPPING
"Save ⅕ of your salary"
"The item is on ½ price sale"

TIME
"It takes 2½ hours" "We're ¾ of the way done"

SHARING/DIVISION
"Each person gets ⅓ of the pizza" "Divide the land into ⅖ and ⅗"

PROBABILITY/STATISTICS "⅔ of students passed" "The chance is 3/10"
Worked Examples:
Example 1: Simplify: 3/8 + 5/12
Solution: Find LCM of 8 and 12: LCM = 24
Convert to equivalent fractions: 3/8 = 9/24 (multiply by 3/3) 5/12 = 10/24 (multiply by 2/2) Add: 9/24 + 10/24 = 19/24
Example 2: Calculate: 4⅔ - 2¾
Solution: Convert to improper fractions: 4⅔ = 14/3 2¾ = 11/4 Find LCM of 3 and 4 = 12
14/3 = 56/12 11/4 = 33/12
Subtract: 56/12 - 33/12 = 23/12 = 1¹¹/₁₂
Example 3: Multiply: 2/5 × 15/8
Solution: Cancel common factors first: 2/5 × 15/8 = 2/5̶ × 1̶5̶/8 = 2/1 × 3/8 (after canceling 5 and 15) = 6/8 = 3/4
Alternative without canceling: 2/5 × 15/8 = 30/40 = 3/4
Example 4: Divide: 3/4 ÷ 5/6
Solution: Use KCF (Keep, Change, Flip): 3/4 ÷ 5/6 = 3/4 × 6/5 = 18/20 = 9/10
Example 5: Solve for x: x/3 + x/4 = 14
Solution: LCM of 3 and 4 = 12
Multiply equation by 12: 12 × x/3 + 12 × x/4 = 12 × 14 4x + 3x = 168 7x = 168 x = 24 Verification: 24/3 + 24/4 = 8 + 6 = 14 ✓
Example 6: A recipe calls for 2¾ cups of flour. If you want to make half the recipe, how much
flour do you need?
Solution: Half of 2¾ = 2¾ ÷ 2 Convert to improper: 2¾ = 11/4
11/4 ÷ 2 = 11/4 × 1/2 = 11/8 = 1⅜ cups
Example 7: John completed ⅖ of his homework on Monday and ⅓ on Tuesday. What fraction remains?
Solution: Total completed = ⅖ + ⅓ LCM of 5 and 3 = 15 ⅖ = 6/15 ⅓ = 5/15 Total completed = 6/15 + 5/15 = 11/15
Remaining = 1 - 11/15 = 15/15 - 11/15 = 4/15
Example 8: A car uses ⅔ liter of fuel to travel 15 km. How much fuel is needed for 45 km?
Solution: 45 km is 3 times 15 km (45 ÷ 15 = 3) Fuel needed = 3 × ⅔ = 3/1 × 2/3 = 6/3 = 2 liters
Alternative method: Fuel per km = ⅔ ÷ 15 = 2/3 × 1/15 = 2/45 liter/km For 45 km: 2/45 × 45 = 2 liters
Example 9: Solve: (2x-1)/5 = (x+3)/3
Solution: Cross multiply: 3(2x - 1) = 5(x + 3) 6x - 3 = 5x + 15 6x - 5x = 15 + 3 x = 18 Verification: Left side: (2(18)-1)/5 = 35/5 = 7 Right side: (18+3)/3 = 21/3 = 7 ✓
Example 10: A container is ⅗ full of water. After using ¼ of what was in the container, what
fraction of the container is now full?
Solution: Amount used = ¼ of ⅗ = ¼ × ⅗ = 3/20 Remaining = ⅗ - 3/20
LCM of 5 and 20 = 20 ⅗ = 12/20 Remaining = 12/20 - 3/20 = 9/20
Example 11: Three siblings share an inheritance. The first gets ½, the second gets ⅓ of the remainder, and the third gets the rest, which is ₦240,000. Find the total inheritance.
Solution: Let total = x
First sibling gets ½x Remainder after first = x - ½x = ½x Second sibling gets ⅓ of ½x = ⅓ × ½x = ⅙x
Third sibling gets = x - ½x - ⅙x
Find common denominator: = 6x/6 - 3x/6 - x/6 = 2x/6 = ⅓x Given that ⅓x = 240,000 x = 240,000 × 3 = ₦720,000 Total inheritance = ₦720,000
Example 12: Simplify the complex fraction: (3/4 + 1/2)/(2/3 - 1/6)
Solution: Numerator: 3/4 + 1/2 = 3/4 + 2/4 = 5/4
Denominator: 2/3 - 1/6 LCM of 3 and 6 = 6 = 4/6 - 1/6 = 3/6 = 1/2 Complex fraction: (5/4)/(1/2) = 5/4 ÷ 1/2 = 5/4 × 2/1 = 10/4 = 5/2 = 2½ Class Activity:
Activity 1: Using fraction strips or circles, demonstrate addition of fractions: ¼ + ⅓
Activity 2: Divide a piece of paper or fruit into fractional parts to show real-life application.
Activity 3: Solve a word problem in groups: "If a student studies ⅖ of the day for Math and ¼ of the day for English, what fraction of the day is left for other activities?"

Content:
Bearings
Definition: A bearing is the direction or path along which something moves or points, measured in degrees clockwise from North.
Key Points:
Bearings are always measured clockwise from North
Bearings are written with three digits (e.g., 045°, 180°, 300°)
North = 000° (or 360°)
East = 090°
South = 180°
West = 270°

Worked Examples:
Example 1: A ship sails from port P on a bearing of 065° for 40 km to point Q. Find: (a) How far east of P is Q (b) How far north of P is Q
Diagram 105:
N
|
P • |
\|65°
\
\40km
\
\
•Q

Need: East (x) and North (y) distances

Solution: (a) Distance east = 40 sin 65° = 40 × 0.9063 = 36.25 km
(b) Distance north = 40 cos 65° = 40 × 0.4226 = 16.90 km
Example 2: Two ships leave a port at the same time. Ship A travels 30 km on a bearing of 040° while Ship B travels 25 km on a bearing of 130°. Find the distance between the two ships.
Diagram 106:
N	N
|	|
|40° A • |
|/	|
Port •	|
|\	|
| \130° |
| \	|
|	\	|
|	• B |

Solution: First, find positions relative to port:
Ship A: East: 30 sin 40° = 30 × 0.6428 = 19.28 km North: 30 cos 40° = 30 × 0.7660 = 22.98 km
Ship B: East: 25 sin 130° = 25 × 0.7660 = 19.15 km South: 25 cos 130° = 25 × (-0.6428) = -
16.07 km (or 16.07 km south)
Difference in East-West: 19.28 - 19.15 = 0.13 km Difference in North-South: 22.98 - (-16.07) =
39.05 km
Distance = √(0.13² + 39.05²) = √(0.0169 + 1524.90) = √1524.92 ≈ 39.05 km
Example 3: A chord of a circle with radius 8 cm subtends an angle of 120° at the center. Find: (a) The length of the chord (b) The perpendicular distance from the center to the chord
Diagram 107:

c
(120° at center)

Solution: (a) Chord length: c = 2r sin(α/2) = 2 × 8 × sin(120°/2) = 16 × sin 60° = 16 × 0.8660 =
13.86 cm
Perpendicular distance: h = r cos(α/2) = 8 × cos 60° = 8 × 0.5 = 4 cm
Example 4: A plane flies from town A to town B on a bearing of 110° for 200 km, then from B to C on a bearing of 200° for 150 km. Find: (a) The distance from A to C (b) The bearing of C from A
Diagram 108:

NNN|||A • |||\|110°||\||200\||\||•B|200°|\||150\||\ ||\||•C|
Solution: Position of B from A: East: 200 sin 110° = 200 × 0.9397 = 187.94 km North: 200 cos 110° = 200 × (-0.3420) = -68.40 km (68.40 km south)
Position of C from B: East: 150 sin 200° = 150 × (-0.3420) = -51.30 km (51.30 km west) North: 150 cos 200° = 150 × (-0.9397) = -140.96 km (140.96 km south)
Position of C from A: Total East: 187.94 - 51.30 = 136.64 km east Total South: 68.40 + 140.96 =
209.36 km south
(a) Distance AC = √(136.64² + 209.36²) = √(18,670.49 + 43,831.62) = √62,502.11 = 250.00 km (b) tan θ = 136.64/209.36 = 0.6527 θ = tan⁻¹(0.6527) = 33.14°
Bearing of C from A = 180° - 33.14° = 146.86° ≈ 147°

Example 5: From a lighthouse L, a ship S is observed on a bearing of 125° at a distance of 8 km. Another ship T is on a bearing of 200° from L at a distance of 6 km. Find the distance between the two ships.
Diagram 109:

•S

Angle SLT = 200° - 125° = 75°

Solution: Using cosine rule in triangle LST: ST² = LS² + LT² - 2(LS)(LT)cos(∠SLT) ST² = 8² + 6² - 2(8)(6)cos 75° ST² = 64 + 36 - 96 × 0.2588 ST² = 100 - 24.84 ST² = 75.16 ST = 8.67 km
Example 6: A town B is 80 km from town A on a bearing of 070°. Town C is 60 km from A on a bearing of 150°. Calculate: (a) The distance BC (b) The bearing of C from B
Diagram 110:
N	N
|	|
|	70° •B

\|	|
\150°	|
\	|

Solution: (a) Angle BAC = 150° - 70° = 80°
Using cosine rule: BC² = AB² + AC² - 2(AB)(AC)cos(∠BAC) BC² = 80² + 60² - 2(80)(60)cos 80° BC² = 6400 + 3600 - 9600 × 0.1736 BC² = 10000 - 1666.56 BC² = 8333.44 BC = 91.29 km
(b) To find bearing of C from B, first find ∠ABC using sine rule:
sin(∠ABC)/AC = sin(∠BAC)/BC sin(∠ABC)/60 = sin 80°/91.29 sin(∠ABC) = (60 × 0.9848)/91.29 sin(∠ABC) = 0.6474 ∠ABC = 40.35°
Bearing of C from B = 180° - 70° + 40.35° = 150.35° ≈ 150°
Example 7: An arc of a circle of radius 12 cm subtends an angle of 75° at the center. Calculate:
The length of the arc (b) The area of the sector (c) The area of the segment
Diagram 111:
•P

/ | sector
12/ |

\		/ arc
Solution: (a) Arc length: s = (θ/360°) × 2πr = (75°/360°) × 2π × 12 = (75/360) × 24π = 5π ≈
15.71 cm
(b) Area of sector: A = (θ/360°) × πr² = (75°/360°) × π × 12² = (75/360) × 144π = 30π ≈ 94.25 cm²
(c) Area of triangle AOB = ½ × 12 × 12 × sin 75° = 72 × 0.9659 = 69.54 cm²
Area of segment = Area of sector - Area of triangle
= 94.25 - 69.54
= 24.71 cm²

Example 8: A helicopter flies 120 km from base B on a bearing of 320°, then changes course to fly on a bearing of 050° for 80 km to reach point P. Find the bearing and distance of P from B.

Diagram 112:

N
|N
||| 50°|80/|/|•P|/320°/|// |	/
120/ |	/
/ |	/
/	| /

Solution: Change in bearing = 50° - (360° - 320°) = 50° - 40° = 10° Angle at turn = 180° - 10° =
170°
Using cosine rule: BP² = 120² + 80² - 2(120)(80)cos 170° BP² = 14400 + 6400 - 19200 × (- 0.9848) BP² = 20800 + 18908.16 BP² = 39708.16 BP = 199.27 km
Using sine rule to find angle at B: sin(∠PBX)/80 = sin 170°/199.27 sin(∠PBX) = (80 × 0.1736)/199.27 sin(∠PBX) = 0.0697 ∠PBX = 4.00°
Bearing of P from B = 320° + 4° = 324°

WEEK 11: REVISION
Learning Objectives:
By the end of the lesson, students should be able to:
Review all topics covered during the second term
Identify and address areas of difficulty
Solve comprehensive problems combining multiple concepts
Demonstrate mastery of term's content
Prepare adequately for terminal examinations
Entry Behaviour:
Students have completed all topics in the second term scheme of work.
Instructional Materials:
Past examination questions
Revision worksheets
Formula charts and theorem summaries
Calculator
Graph papers
Circle geometry instruments
Whiteboard and markers
Content:
Straight Line Graphs
Equation forms: y = mx + c, standard form
Finding gradients and intercepts
Equations from two points or point and gradient
Parallel and perpendicular lines
Drawing tangents to curves
Key Formulas:
Gradient: m = (y₂ - y₁)/(x₂ - x₁)
Point-gradient form: y - y₁ = m(x - x₁)
Parallel lines: m₁ = m₂
Perpendicular lines: m₁ × m₂ = -1
Circle Geometry
Tangent properties (perpendicular to radius)
Equal tangents from external point
Angle in semicircle = 90°
Angle at center = 2 × angle at circumference
Angles in same segment are equal
Cyclic quadrilaterals (opposite angles sum to 180°)
Chord properties
Alternate segment theorem
Trigonometry
Sine rule: a/sin A = b/sin B = c/sin C
Cosine rule: a² = b² + c² - 2bc cos A
Pythagoras' theorem: c² = a² + b²
Angles of elevation and depression
Bearings (three-figure notation)
Chord and arc calculations
Navigation problems
Key Formulas:
SOHCAHTOA
Chord length: c = 2r sin(α/2)
Arc length: s = (θ/360°) × 2πr
Area of sector: A = (θ/360°) × πr²
Fractions
Operations: addition, subtraction, multiplication, division
Mixed numbers and improper fractions
Solving equations with fractions
Word problems and applications
Comprehensive Worked Examples:
Example 1 (Straight Lines): Find the equation of the line passing through (2, -3) and perpendicular to the line 2x + 3y = 6.
Solution: First, find gradient of given line: 2x + 3y = 6 3y = -2x + 6 y = -⅔x + 2 m₁ = -⅔ For perpendicular line: m₂ = -1/m₁ = -1/(-⅔) = 3/2
Using y - y₁ = m(x - x₁) with point (2, -3): y - (-3) = (3/2)(x - 2) y + 3 = (3/2)x - 3 y = (3/2)x - 6 Or: 2y = 3x - 12 3x - 2y = 12
Example 2 (Circle Geometry): In the diagram, PQRS is a cyclic quadrilateral with ∠PQR = 75° and ∠QRS = 95°. A tangent at P makes an angle of 40° with PQ. Find: (a) ∠PSR (b)
∠SPQ (c) ∠PRS
Solution: (a) ∠PSR + ∠PQR = 180° (opposite angles of cyclic quad) ∠PSR + 75° = 180°
∠PSR = 105°
∠SPQ + ∠QRS = 180° (opposite angles of cyclic quad) ∠SPQ + 95° = 180° ∠SPQ = 85°
Using alternate segment theorem: Angle between tangent and PQ = ∠PRS ∠PRS = 40°
Example 3 (Combined Trigonometry): A ship leaves port P and sails 60 km on a bearing of 050° to point Q. From Q, it sails on a bearing of 140° for 40 km to reach point R. Calculate: (a) The distance PR (b) The bearing of R from P
Solution: Angle PQR = 180° - (140° - 50°) = 90°
Since angle is 90°: (a) PR² = PQ² + QR² PR² = 60² + 40² PR² = 3600 + 1600 PR² = 5200 PR =
72.11 km
(b) tan(∠QPR) = QR/PQ = 40/60 = 0.6667 ∠QPR = 33.69°
Bearing of R from P = 50° + 33.69° = 83.69° ≈ 084°

Example 4 (Fractions with Geometry): A circle is divided into three sectors such that the first sector occupies ⅖ of the circle, the second occupies ⅓ of the remainder. If the third sector has an area of 60 cm², find: (a) The area of the circle (b) The radius of the circle
Solution: (a) First sector = ⅖ of circle Remainder = 1 - ⅖ = ⅗
Second sector = ⅓ of ⅗ = ⅓ × ⅗ = ⅕ Third sector = 1 - ⅖ - ⅕
= 5/5 - 2/5 - 1/5
= 2/5

If ⅖ = 60 cm²
Total area = 60 ÷ (2/5) = 60 × 5/2 = 150 cm²

(b) πr² = 150 r² = 150/π = 47.75 r = 6.91 cm
Example 5 (Complex Circle Problem): A chord AB of a circle with center O and radius 13 cm is 10 cm from the center. Calculate: (a) The length of the chord (b) The angle subtended by the chord at the center (c) The area of the minor segment
Diagram 113:
•P
/|\
/ | \
/ | \
13 /	|10 \13
/	|	\
/	|		\ A•		|		•B
O •
(M is midpoint)

Solution: (a) Let M be foot of perpendicular from O to AB In right triangle OMA: AM² = OA² - OM² AM² = 13² - 10² AM² = 169 - 100 = 69 AM = 8.31 cm
AB = 2 × AM = 16.62 cm

(b) cos(α/2) = OM/OA = 10/13 = 0.7692 α/2 = 39.74° α = 79.48°
(c) Area of sector = (79.48°/360°) × π × 13² = 0.2208 × 530.93 = 117.23 cm²
Area of triangle AOB = ½ × 13 × 13 × sin 79.48°
= 84.5 × 0.9843
= 83.17 cm²

Area of segment = 117.23 - 83.17 = 34.06 cm²

Example 6 (Multi-Step Problem): Two straight lines L₁: 3x - 4y = 12 and L₂: 2x + y = 5 intersect at point P. Find: (a) The coordinates of P (b) The acute angle between the two lines (c) The equation of a line through P perpendicular to L₁
Solution: (a) Solve simultaneously: From L₂: y = 5 - 2x
Substitute into L₁: 3x - 4(5 - 2x) = 12
3x - 20 + 8x = 12
11x = 32 x = 32/11
y = 5 - 2(32/11) = 55/11 - 64/11 = -9/11

P = (32/11, -9/11) or (2.91, -0.82)

Find gradients: L₁: y = ¾x - 3, m₁ = ¾ L₂: y = -2x + 5, m₂ = -2
tan θ = |(m₁ - m₂)/(1 + m₁m₂)|
= |(3/4 - (-2))/(1 + (3/4)(-2))|
= |(11/4)/(1 - 3/2)|
= |(11/4)/(-1/2)|
= |-11/2|
= 5.5

θ = tan⁻¹(5.5) = 79.70°

Gradient perpendicular to L₁ = -1/(3/4) = -4/3
Using y - y₁ = m(x - x₁):
y - (-9/11) = -4/3(x - 32/11) y + 9/11 = -4/3x + 128/33

Multiply by 33:
33y + 27 = -44x + 128
44x + 33y = 101

Two lines L₁ : 2x - 3y = 6 and L₂ : 4x + ky = 8 are perpendicular. Find: (a) The value of k (b) The coordinates of their point of intersection (c) The distance from the origin to the point of intersection
In a circle with center O and radius 15 cm: (a) A chord AB is 18 cm from O. Find its length. (b) The chord subtends an angle at the center. Find this angle. (c) If a tangent at A makes an angle of 35° with AB, find the angle in the alternate segment.

Calculate: (a) The horizontal distance from the boat to the base of the cliff (b) The direct distance from the top of the cliff to the boat (c) If the boat moves directly toward the cliff at 5 m/s, how long before the angle of depression becomes 45°?

SS2 MATHEMATICS LESSON NOTES
THIRD TERM

WEEK 1: INTRODUCTION TO LINEAR PROGRAMMING
Learning Objectives:
By the end of the lesson, students should be able to:
Define linear programming and its components
Identify real-life situations requiring linear programming
Formulate linear programming problems from word problems
Identify constraints and objective functions
Understand the terminology used in linear programming
Entry Behaviour:
Students can solve linear equations and inequalities, plot points on coordinate planes, and understand basic algebraic expressions.
Instructional Materials:
Whiteboard and markers
Graph papers
Real-life scenario cards
Charts showing linear programming applications
Calculator
Business case study examples
Content:
Meaning of Linear Programming
Definition: Linear programming (LP) is a mathematical method used to find the best possible outcome (maximum profit, minimum cost, etc.) in a given mathematical model with linear relationships.
Key Components:
Diagram 1: Linear Programming Framework
LINEAR PROGRAMMING PROBLEM

DECISION VARIABLES Variables we can control Usually: x, y, z, etc.
Example: x = number of product A y = number of product B

OBJECTIVE FUNCTION
What we want to maximize or minimize
Format: Z = ax + by (to maximize or minimize) Example: Maximize Profit: P = 5x + 3y

CONSTRAINTS
Limitations or restrictions Format: Linear inequalities
Example: x + 2y ≤ 100 (resource limit) x ≥ 0, y ≥ 0 (non-negativity)

FEASIBLE REGION
Area containing all possible solutions that satisfy all constraints

OPTIMAL SOLUTION
Best solution within feasible region
Maximum or minimum value of objective function

Why "Linear"?
All relationships are expressed as linear equations or inequalities
Variables appear to the first power only (no x², xy, √x, etc.)
Graphs are straight lines
Diagram 2: Linear vs Non-Linear
LINEAR EXAMPLES:
2x + 3y ≤ 100
x + y = 50
5x - 2y ≥ 20

NON-LINEAR EXAMPLES:
✗ x² + y ≤ 100	(has x²)
✗ xy = 50	(has xy)
✗ √x + y ≥ 20	(has √x)

Formulation of Linear Programming Problems
Steps to Formulate LP Problems:
Diagram 3: Formulation Process
STEP 1: IDENTIFY DECISION VARIABLES
↓
What quantities can we choose or control? Define: Let x = ..., y = ...

STEP 2: WRITE THE OBJECTIVE FUNCTION
↓
What are we trying to maximize or minimize? Write: Maximize/Minimize Z = ...

STEP 3: IDENTIFY CONSTRAINTS
↓
What are the limitations or restrictions? List all inequalities

STEP 4: ADD NON-NEGATIVITY CONSTRAINTS
↓
Usually: x ≥ 0, y ≥ 0
(Can't produce negative quantities)

STEP 5: SUMMARIZE THE PROBLEM
↓
Present complete formulation

Key Terms:
Diagram 4: LP Terminology
┌─────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ OBJECTIVE FUNCTION	│
│ Function to be optimized (max or min)	│
│ Example: Maximize P = 50x + 40y	│
└─────────────────────────────────────────┘

┌─────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ CONSTRAINTS	│
│ Conditions that must be satisfied	│
│ Example: 2x + y ≤ 100	│
│	x + 3y ≤ 150	│
└─────────────────────────────────────────┘

┌─────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ FEASIBLE SOLUTION	│
│ Any solution satisfying all constraints │
│ May not be optimal	│
└─────────────────────────────────────────┘

┌─────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ OPTIMAL SOLUTION	│
│ Best feasible solution	│
│ Maximizes or minimizes objective	│
└─────────────────────────────────────────┘

┌─────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ FEASIBLE REGION	│
│ Set of all feasible solutions	│
│ Shown as shaded area on graph	│
└─────────────────────────────────────────┘

Real-Life Applications of Linear Programming
Common Applications:
Diagram 5: LP Application Areas
MANUFACTURING
Problem: Product mix optimization Example: How many units of each product
to maximize profit?

Factory
┌─────────────────┐
│ Machine A	│ → Product 1
│ Machine B	│ → Product 2
│ Limited time	│
│ Limited material│
└─────────────────┘

AGRICULTURE
Problem: Crop planning
Example: How much of each crop to plant to maximize profit?

Farm
┌─────────────────┐
│ Limited land	│ → Crop A
│ Limited water	│ → Crop B
│ Limited labor	│ → Crop C
└─────────────────┘

DIET PLANNING (Nutrition) Problem: Minimum cost diet
Example: Meet nutritional requirements at minimum cost

Meal Plan
┌─────────────────┐
│ Protein ≥ 50g	│ → Food 1
│ Carbs ≥ 200g	│ → Food 2
│ Cost minimized │ → Food 3
└─────────────────┘

TRANSPORTATION
Problem: Route optimization
Example: Minimize transportation cost
Delivery
┌─────────────────┐
│ From warehouses │ → To shops
│ Limited capacity│
│ Minimize cost	│
└─────────────────┘

RESOURCE ALLOCATION
Problem: Assign resources efficiently Example: Allocate budget, staff, equipment

INVESTMENT
Problem: Portfolio optimization
Example: Maximize return with risk limits

Nigerian Context Examples:
Diagram 6: Local Applications
FARMING
"Farmer Musa has 10 hectares. Should he plant rice or cassava to maximize profit?"

MARKET TRADING
"Mama Ngozi has ₦50,000 to buy goods. What mix of items gives maximum profit?"

FUEL DISTRIBUTION
"A filling station wants to determine optimal stock of petrol and diesel"

SCHOOL RESOURCES
"How to allocate budget between science
and art equipment to maximize students' needs?"

SMALL BUSINESS
"A tailor can make dresses or shirts. Which combination maximizes daily profit?"
Worked Examples:
Example 1: A factory produces two types of chairs: dining chairs and office chairs. Each dining chair requires 2 hours of labor and 3 kg of wood. Each office chair requires 3 hours of labor and 2 kg of wood. The factory has 120 hours of labor and 150 kg of wood available per week. Define the decision variables and constraints (do not solve).
Solution:
Step 1: Define Decision Variables Let x = number of dining chairs produced per week Let y = number of office chairs produced per week
Step 2: Write Objective Function (not given, but typically would be profit) Suppose profit is
₦5,000 per dining chair and ₦6,000 per office chair Maximize P = 5000x + 6000y
Step 3: Identify Constraints
Labor constraint: 2x + 3y ≤ 120 (hours available) Wood constraint: 3x + 2y ≤ 150 (kg available) Non-negativity: x ≥ 0, y ≥ 0
Complete Formulation:
Maximize P = 5000x + 6000y Subject to:
2x + 3y ≤ 120
3x + 2y ≤ 150 x ≥ 0, y ≥ 0
Example 2: A farmer has 100 hectares of land. He can plant rice or maize. Rice requires 2 workers per hectare and maize requires 1 worker per hectare. He has 150 workers available. Rice yields a profit of ₦80,000 per hectare and maize yields ₦50,000 per hectare. Formulate this as a linear programming problem.
Solution:
Decision Variables: Let x = number of hectares planted with rice Let y = number of hectares planted with maize
Objective Function: Maximize Profit: P = 80,000x + 50,000y
Constraints: Land constraint: x + y ≤ 100 (total land available) Labor constraint: 2x + y ≤ 150 (total workers available) Non-negativity: x ≥ 0, y ≥ 0
Complete Formulation:
Maximize P = 80,000x + 50,000y Subject to:
x + y ≤ 100 2x + y ≤ 150 x ≥ 0, y ≥ 0
Example 3: A baker makes cakes and bread. Each cake takes 2 hours to bake and uses 3 kg of flour. Each bread takes 1 hour to bake and uses 2 kg of flour. The baker has 40 hours and 60 kg of flour available. Each cake sells for ₦2,000 and each bread for ₦800. Formulate the problem.
Solution:
Decision Variables: Let x = number of cakes to make Let y = number of breads to make
Objective Function: Maximize Revenue: R = 2000x + 800y
Constraints: Time constraint: 2x + y ≤ 40 (hours available) Flour constraint: 3x + 2y ≤ 60 (kg available) Non-negativity: x ≥ 0, y ≥ 0
Complete Formulation:
Maximize R = 2000x + 800y Subject to:
2x + y ≤ 40
3x + 2y ≤ 60 x ≥ 0, y ≥ 0
Example 4: A company produces two products A and B. Product A requires 4 hours on Machine 1 and 2 hours on Machine 2. Product B requires 2 hours on Machine 1 and 3 hours on Machine 2. Machine 1 is available for 80 hours and Machine 2 for 60 hours per week. The profit per unit is
₦3,000 for A and ₦2,500 for B. Formulate the LP problem.
Solution:
Decision Variables: Let x = number of units of Product A Let y = number of units of Product B
Objective Function: Maximize Profit: P = 3000x + 2500y
Constraints: Machine 1: 4x + 2y ≤ 80 (hours available) Machine 2: 2x + 3y ≤ 60 (hours available) Non-negativity: x ≥ 0, y ≥ 0
Complete Formulation:
Maximize P = 3000x + 2500y Subject to:
4x + 2y ≤ 80
2x + 3y ≤ 60 x ≥ 0, y ≥ 0
Example 5: A nutritionist is planning a diet that must contain at least 300 calories and 50g of protein. Food A provides 100 calories and 20g of protein per serving, costing ₦200. Food B provides 150 calories and 10g of protein per serving, costing ₦250. Formulate to minimize cost.
Solution:
Decision Variables: Let x = number of servings of Food A Let y = number of servings of Food B
Objective Function: Minimize Cost: C = 200x + 250y
Constraints: Calorie requirement: 100x + 150y ≥ 300 (at least 300 calories) Protein requirement: 20x + 10y ≥ 50 (at least 50g protein) Non-negativity: x ≥ 0, y ≥ 0
Complete Formulation:
Minimize C = 200x + 250y Subject to:
100x + 150y ≥ 300
20x + 10y ≥ 50 x ≥ 0, y ≥ 0
Note: This is a minimization problem with "≥" constraints (minimum requirements).
Example 6: A tailor makes traditional wear (agbada) and modern suits. Making an agbada takes 4 hours and uses 6 meters of fabric. Making a suit takes 3 hours and uses 4 meters of fabric. The tailor works 60 hours per week and has 90 meters of fabric. Profit is ₦15,000 per agbada and
₦12,000 per suit. Formulate this problem.
Solution:
Decision Variables: Let x = number of agbada to make per week Let y = number of suits to make per week
Objective Function: Maximize Profit: P = 15,000x + 12,000y
Constraints: Time: 4x + 3y ≤ 60 Fabric: 6x + 4y ≤ 90 Non-negativity: x ≥ 0, y ≥ 0
Complete Formulation:
Maximize P = 15,000x + 12,000y Subject to:
4x + 3y ≤ 60
6x + 4y ≤ 90 x ≥ 0, y ≥ 0
Example 7: Identify which of the following are linear programming problems:
Maximize Z = 3x + 2y Subject to: x² + y ≤ 10, x ≥ 0, y ≥ 0
Minimize C = 5x + 4y Subject to: 2x + 3y ≥ 12, x + y ≤ 8, x ≥ 0, y ≥ 0
Maximize P = xy Subject to: x + y ≤ 20, x ≥ 0, y ≥ 0
Solution:
NOT Linear Programming - contains x² (non-linear term)
IS Linear Programming - all equations/inequalities are linear, objective function is linear
NOT Linear Programming - objective function contains xy (non-linear term)
Class Activity:
Activity 1: In groups, identify decision variables for this scenario: "A school wants to buy computers and projectors. Computers cost ₦150,000 each and projectors cost ₦80,000 each. They have ₦2,000,000 budget."
Activity 2: Write constraints for: "A bus company has 10 buses. Each bus needs 2 drivers and 1 mechanic per trip. There are 15 drivers and 8 mechanics available."
Activity 3: Create your own real-life scenario that could be solved using linear programming.
Evaluation Questions:
Define linear programming and state its main components.

What is the difference between an objective function and a constraint?

A company makes products X and Y. Product X takes 2 hours and product Y takes 3 hours. There are 120 hours available. Write the time constraint.
State three real-life applications of linear programming.

Why must decision variables in most LP problems be non-negative?

Assignment:
A farmer plants tomatoes and peppers. Tomatoes need 3 hours of labor per hectare and peppers need 2 hours per hectare. He has 60 hours of labor available per week. Tomatoes give ₦40,000 profit per hectare and peppers give ₦30,000. He has 25 hectares of land. Formulate this as an LP problem (define variables, objective function, and all constraints).
Explain the meaning of the following terms in linear programming: (a) Decision variables (b) Feasible solution
(c) Optimal solution (d) Constraint (e) Objective function
Which of these are linear programming problems? Explain why or why not: (a) Maximize P = 4x + 5y; subject to 2x + 3y ≤ 100, x ≥ 0, y ≥ 0 (b) Maximize P = x² + y; subject to x + y ≤ 50, x ≥ 0, y ≥ 0 (c) Minimize C = 3x + 7y; subject to x + 2y ≥ 20, 3x + y ≥ 30, x ≥ 0, y ≥ 0
A baker makes meat pies and sausage rolls. Each meat pie requires 2 eggs and 100g flour, costing ₦300 to make and selling for ₦500. Each sausage roll requires 1 egg and 50g flour, costing ₦150 to make and selling for ₦300. The baker has 100 eggs and 5kg (5000g) flour available daily. Formulate this problem to maximize profit.
Give two examples of situations in your community or school that could be modeled using linear programming.

WEEK 2: LINEAR PROGRAMMING - GRAPHICAL SOLUTIONS
Learning Objectives:
By the end of the lesson, students should be able to:
Graph linear inequalities on the coordinate plane
Identify and shade feasible regions
Find corner points of feasible regions
Solve optimization problems graphically
Interpret solutions in context of original problems
Entry Behaviour:
Students can formulate LP problems, plot straight lines, understand inequalities, and identify coordinates of points.
Instructional Materials:
Graph papers (plenty)
Rulers and pencils
Colored pencils for shading
Calculator
Whiteboard and markers
Pre-drawn example graphs

Types of Lines:
Diagram 8: Solid vs Dashed Lines
INEQUALITY	LINE TYPE	MEANING
─────────────────────────────────────

≤ or ≥Solid ─────Points on line
ARE included< or >Dashed - - -Points on line NOT includedExamples:x + y ≤ 6	Solid line (points on line OK)
x + y < 6	Dashed line (points on line NOT OK)

Shading Rules:
Diagram 9: Which Side to Shade
METHOD 1: Test Point Method
Draw the line
Choose test point (0,0) if not on line
Substitute into inequality
If TRUE → shade side with test point If FALSE → shade opposite side

METHOD 2: Understanding Inequality
"≤" or "<" : Shade BELOW/LEFT
"≥" or ">" : Shade ABOVE/RIGHT

Example: y ≤ 2x + 3

Feasible Region
Definition: The feasible region is the area that satisfies ALL constraints simultaneously. It's the intersection of all constraint regions.
Diagram 10: Finding Feasible Region
Example: Find feasible region for: x + y ≤ 6
2x + y ≤ 10 x ≥ 0, y ≥ 0
STEP 1: Graph each constraint Line 1: x + y = 6
Points: (0,6) and (6,0)

The feasible region is polygon ABCD

Finding Intersection Points:
Diagram 11: Calculating Corner Points
To find point C where lines intersect:
x + y = 6	... (1)
2x + y = 10 ... (2)

Solve simultaneously:
From (1): y = 6 - x Substitute into (2):
2x + (6 - x) = 10
2x + 6 - x = 10
x = 4

Substitute back:
y = 6 - 4 = 2

Therefore C = (4, 2)

[Note: There was an error in Diagram 10 above. The correct intersection is C = (4, 2)]
Solving Optimization Problems Graphically
Corner Point Theorem: The optimal solution of a linear programming problem (if it exists) occurs at a corner point (vertex) of the feasible region.
Steps to Solve LP Graphically: Diagram 12: Solution Process
STEP 1: Graph all constraints
↓
STEP 2: Identify feasible region
↓
STEP 3: Find all corner points
↓
STEP 4: Evaluate objective function at each corner point
↓
STEP 5: Select optimal value (Maximum or Minimum)
↓
STEP 6: State the optimal solution

Types of Solutions:
Diagram 13: Possible Outcomes
UNIQUE OPTIMAL SOLUTION One corner gives best value
y
| •Optimal
| /\
| / \
|/	\
• x

MULTIPLE OPTIMAL SOLUTIONS
Two or more corners give same best value y
| •═══• Both optimal
| /\
|/ \
•	• x

UNBOUNDED SOLUTION
Objective function can increase indefinitely y
|	/
| / ← Feasible region
| /	extends infinitely
|/
 	 x
NO FEASIBLE SOLUTION
Constraints cannot all be satisfied y
| \ / ← Constraints
| \/	don't overlap
| /\
 	 x

Example 3: A factory makes chairs and tables. Each chair takes 2 hours and each table takes 4 hours to make. There are 80 hours available. Each chair needs 3 kg wood and each table needs 5 kg wood. There are 120 kg wood available. Profit is ₦2,000 per chair and ₦3,000 per table. How many of each should be made to maximize profit?
Solution:
Formulation: Let x = number of chairs Let y = number of tables Maximize P = 2000x + 3000y
Subject to:
2x + 4y ≤ 80 (time)
3x + 5y ≤ 120 (wood)
x ≥ 0, y ≥ 0
Simplify first constraint: 2x + 4y ≤ 80 Divide by 2: x + 2y ≤ 40

For intersection: x + 2y = 40 → 5x + 10y = 200 3x + 5y = 120 → 6x + 10y = 240 Subtract: -x = -40, so x = 40... wait
Let me use elimination properly: x + 2y = 40 ... ×5 3x + 5y = 120 ... ×2 5x + 10y = 200 6x + 10y = 240
Subtract: -x = -40 x = 40, then y = 0
The lines intersect at the same point (40, 0).
Actually, the second constraint is less restrictive at that point. Let me find C differently. Actually, corner C is where x + 2y = 40 meets 3x + 5y = 120
Multiply equation 1 by 5: 5x + 10y = 200 Multiply equation 2 by 2: 6x + 10y = 240 Subtract: x = 40... I keep getting the same answer.
Let me try substitution: From x + 2y = 40: x = 40 - 2y Into 3x + 5y = 120: 3(40-2y) + 5y = 120 120 - 6y + 5y = 120 -y = 0 y = 0, x = 40
The constraints meet at D. So the feasible region has only 3 distinct corners: A, B, D.
Evaluate P = 2000x + 3000y:

PointxyP = 2000x + 3000yValueA0000B0202000(0) + 3000(20)60,000D4002000(40) + 3000(0)80,000Answer: Maximum profit = ₦80,000 when making 40 chairs and 0 tables.
Example 4: Graph the feasible region for:
x ≥ 2
y ≥ 1
x + y ≤ 8
x, y ≥ 0
Diagram 17: Bounded Feasible Region

x

0128
A =
(2,
1)
[x=2 meets y=1]B =(7,1)[x+y=8 meets y=1]C =(2,6)[x=2 meets x+y=8]
Solution: Corner points:
A = (2, 1)
B: When y = 1, x + 1 = 8, so x = 7. B = (7, 1)
C: When x = 2, 2 + y = 8, so y = 6. C = (2, 6) Feasible region is triangle ABC.

He has 25 hectares of land. (a) Formulate as LP problem (b) Solve graphically (c) State how many hectares of each crop to plant

WEEK 3: BEARINGS
Learning Objectives:
By the end of the lesson, students should be able to:
Define bearings and explain their importance
Distinguish between compass bearings and three-figure bearings
Convert between different bearing notations
Solve problems involving bearings
Apply bearings to navigation and real-life situations
Entry Behaviour:
Students understand compass directions (North, South, East, West), can measure and draw angles, and understand the concept of clockwise direction.
Instructional Materials:
Compass (magnetic and drawing)
Protractor
Ruler
Whiteboard and markers
Maps showing bearing examples
Calculator
Directional cards
Content:
Definition and Types of Bearings
Definition: A bearing is the direction of one point relative to another point, measured as an angle.
Key Principles:
Bearings are always measured from North
Bearings are always measured clockwise
Bearings describe direction, not distance
Diagram 18: The North Reference
N (North)
↑
|
| Always start
| from North
|
W ←--•--→ E
|
|
↓ S

Every bearing measurement starts from the North line and goes clockwise
Three-Figure Bearings (3-Digit Bearings)
Format: Always written with exactly three digits, from 000° to 360°
Diagram 19: Three-Figure Bearings
N (000°/360°)
|
|
|
W	•		E (270°)|	(090°)
|
|
S (180°)

CARDINAL DIRECTIONS:
- North: 000° (or 360°)
- East: 090°
South: 180°
- West: 270°

EXAMPLES:
N	N
|	|
|045°	|
|/	\|135°
/|	|\
/ |	| \

045° bearing	135° bearing (Northeast)	(Southeast)

N	N
|	|
|\	|/
| \	/|
| \225°	315°|
|	|

225° bearing	315° bearing (Southwest)	(Northwest)
Writing Three-Figure Bearings: Diagram 20: Bearing Notation RULE: Always use THREE digits
Angle	Written as	Examples
──────────────────────────────
Very important!

or 000°

INCORRECT: 45°, 5°, 90°
CORRECT:	045°, 005°, 090°
Compass Bearings (4-Point Bearings)
Format: Written using compass directions: N, S, E, W with an angle
Diagram 21: Compass Bearing Notation
QUADRANT SYSTEM:

NW	N	NE
\	|	/
\ | / W ---•--- E
/ | \
/	|	\ SW	S		SE

NOTATION RULES:
Start with N or S (never E or W)
State the angle
End with E or W

Format: [N or S] [angle] [E or W] EXAMPLES:
N	N
|	|
|45°	\|
|/	|\
/|	|30°\
/ |	|	\

N 45° E	S 30° W
or	or
N45°E	S30°W

Conversion Between Bearing Types
Three-Figure to Compass Bearing:
Diagram 23: Conversion Rules
THREE-FIGURE	→	COMPASS BEARING
────────────────────────────────────

000° to 090° →	N	°E
(1st Quadrant) Example: 040° → N40°E
────────────────────────────────────

090° to 180° →	S	°E
(2nd Quadrant)	Angle from South = 180° - bearing

Example: 120° → 180° - 120° = 60°
· S60°E
──────────────────────────────────── 180° to 270° →	S	°W
(3rd Quadrant)	Angle from South = bearing - 180°

Example: 210° → 210° - 180° = 30°
· S30°W
──────────────────────────────────── 270° to 360° →	N	°W
(4th Quadrant)	Angle from North = 360° - bearing

Example: 320° → 360° - 320° = 40°
· N40°W

Compass to Three-Figure Bearing:
Diagram 24: Reverse Conversion
COMPASS	→	THREE-FIGURE
────────────────────────────────

N	°E	→	Same angle Example: N40°E → 040°

S	°E	→	180° - angle Example: S60°E → 180° - 60° = 120°

S	°W	→	180° + angle Example: S30°W → 180° + 30° = 210°

N	°W	→	360° - angle Example: N40°W → 360° - 40° = 320°
Back Bearings (Return Bearings)
Definition: The bearing from point B back to point A, when the bearing from A to B is known.
Diagram 25: Back Bearings
RULE: Back bearing differs by 180°

If bearing < 180°:
Back bearing = bearing + 180°

If bearing ≥ 180°:
Back bearing = bearing - 180°

EXAMPLE 1:
N	N
|	|
A • |	|
\|050°	|
\	230°|\
\	| \
\	| •B
B	|

Forward: A to B = 050°
Back:	B to A = 050° + 180° = 230°

EXAMPLE 2:
N	N
|	|
|	A •|
|\	/|
| \245° / |065°
| \	/ |
|	\ /	|
|	•B	|

Forward: A to B = 245°
Back:	B to A = 245° - 180° = 065°

Solving Bearing Problems
Problem-Solving Steps:
Diagram 26: Problem-Solving Framework
STEP 1: Draw a clear diagram
Mark North at each point
Label all points
Show given bearings

STEP 2: Identify what is given
Bearings
Distances
Angles

STEP 3: Determine what to find
Distance?
Bearing?
Position?

STEP 4: Use appropriate method
Trigonometry (sine, cosine, tan)
Pythagoras theorem
Properties of angles

STEP 5: Calculate and verify
Check reasonableness
Verify bearing is 000°-360°
Check units

Worked Examples:
Example 1: Convert the following bearings: (a) 055° to compass bearing (b) N65°W to three- figure bearing (c) 215° to compass bearing (d) S20°E to three-figure bearing
Solution:
055° is in 1st quadrant (000° to 090°) Compass bearing = N55°E
N65°W is in 4th quadrant Three-figure = 360° - 65° = 295°
215° is in 3rd quadrant (180° to 270°) Angle from South = 215° - 180° = 35° Compass bearing = S35°W
S20°E is in 2nd quadrant Three-figure = 180° - 20° = 160°
Example 2: Point B is on a bearing of 120° from point A. Find the bearing of A from B.

Diagram 27:
N	N
|	|
A • |	|
|\	|
| \120°	|
| \	|
|	\	|\
|	\	| \300°
|	\	| \
|	•B |	•A

Solution: Bearing of A from B (back bearing): Since 120° < 180°: Back bearing = 120° + 180° = 300°
Answer: Bearing of A from B = 300°
Example 3: A ship sails from port P on a bearing of 040° for 80 km to reach point Q. Find: (a) How far east Q is from P (b) How far north Q is from P
Diagram 28:
N
|
P • |
\|40°
\ 80\km
\
\
•Q

Need: Eastward and Northward distances

Solution:
(a) Distance east = 80 sin 40° = 80 × 0.6428 = 51.42 km
(b) Distance north = 80 cos 40° = 80 × 0.7660 = 61.28 km
Example 4: Town B is 50 km from town A on a bearing of 135°. Town C is 40 km from A on a bearing of 225°. Find the distance BC.
Diagram 29:
N	N
|	|
|	|
A • |	|
\|135°	|
50\	|
\	|
B	|\
| \225°
|40\
|	\
|	•C

Angle BAC = 225° - 135° = 90°

Solution: Angle between the two bearings = 225° - 135° = 90°
Since angle = 90°, use Pythagoras: BC² = AB² + AC² BC² = 50² + 40² BC² = 2500 + 1600 BC² = 4100 BC = 64.03 km
Example 5: A plane flies from airport X to airport Y on a bearing of 065° for 200 km. It then flies from Y to Z on a bearing of 155° for 150 km. Find: (a) The distance XZ (b) The bearing of Z from X
Diagram 30:
N	N
|	|
X • |	|
\|65°	|
200\	|
\	|
Y	|
\	|155°
150\	|
\	|
\	|
Z	|

Angle at Y = 180° - (155° - 65°)
= 180° - 90° = 90°

Solution:
(a) Angle XYZ = 180° - (155° - 65°) = 90°
Using Pythagoras:
XZ² = XY² + YZ² XZ² = 200² + 150²
XZ² = 40,000 + 22,500
XZ² = 62,500 XZ = 250 km
(b) To find bearing of Z from X: tan(angle) = YZ/XY = 150/200 = 0.75 angle = tan⁻¹(0.75) = 36.87°
Bearing of Z from X = 65° + 36.87° = 101.87°
≈ 102°

Example 6: From a port P, a ship sails 60 km on bearing N30°E to point Q. From Q, it sails 80 km on bearing S60°E to point R. Find the distance PR.
Diagram 31:
N	N
|	|
|30°	|
|/	|
P •_/	|
/ \60	|
/	\	|
/	•Q	|
/	\	|60°
/	80\	|\
/	\	| \
/	\ | \
•R

Analysis of angles needed

Solution: Convert bearings to angles:
N30°E from P = 030°
S60°E from Q means 60° from South towards East
At Q, the internal angle: External bearing from Q = 180° - 60° = 120° from North Angle PQR = 180° - 30° - 60° = 90°
Since angle = 90°: PR² = PQ² + QR² PR² = 60² + 80² PR² = 3,600 + 6,400 PR² = 10,000 PR =
100 km
Example 7: Point A is 100 km from point B on a bearing of 240°. What is the bearing of B from A?
Solution: Bearing of B from A (back bearing): Since 240° > 180°: Back bearing = 240° - 180° = 060°
Answer: Bearing of B from A = 060°
Example 8: Convert the following: (a) 315° to compass bearing (b) S75°W to three-figure bearing
Solution:
315° is in 4th quadrant (270° to 360°) Angle from North = 360° - 315° = 45° Compass bearing = N45°W
S75°W is in 3rd quadrant Three-figure = 180° + 75° = 255°
Example 9: A boat leaves harbor H and sails 30 km on bearing 050° to buoy B. From B, the boat is on a bearing of 140° from a lighthouse L, which is due east of H. Find the distance HL.

Diagram 32:
N
N
N|||H • ||| L\|50°|140°|\30\	|	/ | \
\	|	/	| \
B	| /	|
|/	|

L is due east of H means bearing = 090°

Solution: From H to L: bearing = 090° (due east) From H to B: bearing = 050° Angle LHB = 90° - 50° = 40°
From L to B: bearing = 140° Back bearing (B to L) = 140° + 180° = 320° This means from B, L is at 320°
In triangle HBL: Angle HBL = 320° - 050° = 270°... [needs recalculation]
Actually, let me reconsider. If L is due east of H (bearing 090°), and B is at bearing 050° from H, and L sees B at bearing 140°:
The bearing of B from L (back bearing of 140°) = 140° - 180° = -40° or 320°
Using sine rule in triangle HBL: This problem requires more geometric analysis. For SS2 level, simpler versions should be used.
Example 10: A helicopter flies from base B on bearing N40°E for 80 km to point P. Convert this bearing to three-figure notation.
Solution: N40°E is in 1st quadrant Three-figure bearing = 040° The bearing is 040°.

WEEK 4: ANGLES OF ELEVATION AND DEPRESSION
Learning Objectives:
By the end of the lesson, students should be able to:
Define angles of elevation and depression
Distinguish between angles of elevation and depression
Construct diagrams showing these angles
Apply trigonometric ratios to solve problems
Solve real-life problems involving heights and distances
Entry Behaviour:
Students understand trigonometric ratios (SOHCAHTOA), can draw angles, and are familiar with right-angled triangles.
Instructional Materials:
Clinometer (or protractor for demonstration)
Ruler and protractor
Pictures of buildings, towers, mountains
String and weight (to make simple clinometer)
Calculator
Whiteboard and markers

Meaning of Angle of Elevation
Definition: The angle of elevation is the angle between the horizontal line (level with the observer's eye) and the line of sight when looking UP at an object.

Relationship Between Elevation and Depression
Important Property: The angle of elevation from point A to point B equals the angle of depression from point B to point A (alternate angles).
Diagram 37: Alternate Angles Property
Observer B (on tower)

 		/| ← Horizontal at B α	/ |
/ |
/	| Height
/ α | ← Angles are equal
/	| (alternate angles)
/	|
 	|
Observer A Horizontal at A

∠ of elevation from A = α
∠ of depression from B = α

WHY? Alternate angles between parallel lines (both horizontals are parallel)
Proof Using Parallel Lines:
Diagram 38: Parallel Lines
Horizontal at B ═════════
\α	‖
\	‖
\	‖
\	‖
α \	‖
\	‖
Horizontal at A ═════\═══

The two horizontal lines are parallel. The line of sight is a transversal.
Therefore, alternate angles are equal. α (elevation) = α (depression)

Trigonometric Ratios to Use:
Diagram 42: SOHCAHTOA Application
Object
•

Observer Distance (d)

tan θ = Opposite/Adjacent = h/d sin θ = Opposite/Hypotenuse = h/s cos θ = Adjacent/Hypotenuse = d/s
MOST COMMON:
tan θ = h/d

Therefore:
h = d × tan θ (finding height) d = h/tan θ (finding distance) θ = tan⁻¹(h/d) (finding angle)
Worked Examples:
Example 1: From a point 50 m from the base of a building, the angle of elevation to the top is 32°. Find the height of the building.
Diagram 43:
Building
|
h |
|
| Observer	|	|
32°
· 50m →

Solution: Using tan θ = opposite/adjacent tan 32° = h/50 h = 50 × tan 32° h = 50 × 0.6249 h = 31.25 m
Answer: Height of building = 31.25 m
Example 2: From the top of a lighthouse 60 m high, the angle of depression to a boat is 25°. How far is the boat from the base of the lighthouse?
Diagram 44:
Lighthouse • 		 60m	\	|
\	|60m
25\	|
\ |

\|
Boat •
· d →
Solution: Angle of depression = 25° Height = 60 m Using tan θ = opposite/adjacent tan 25° = 60/d
d = 60/tan 25° d = 60/0.4663 d = 128.65 m
Answer: Distance = 128.65 m
Example 3: A ladder 10 m long leans against a wall. If the ladder makes an angle of 65° with the ground, how high up the wall does it reach?
Diagram 45:
Wall
|\
| \
h | \10m
|	\
|	\
|	65°\ Ground
Solution: Using sin θ = opposite/hypotenuse sin 65° = h/10 h = 10 × sin 65° h = 10 × 0.9063 h = 9.063 m
Answer: Height = 9.06 m
Example 4: From a point on the ground, the angle of elevation to the top of a tower is 30°. From a point 40 m closer to the tower, the angle of elevation is 45°. Find the height of the tower.
Diagram 46:
Tower
|
h |
|
Point A	|	Point B	|
30°	40m	45°
←—— d ——→

Solution: Let distance from B to tower = x Then distance from A to tower = x + 40 From point B: tan 45° = h/x 1 = h/x (since tan 45° = 1) h = x ... (1)
From point A: tan 30° = h/(x + 40) 0.5774 = h/(x + 40) h = 0.5774(x + 40) ... (2)
Substitute (1) into (2): x = 0.5774(x + 40) x = 0.5774x + 23.096 x - 0.5774x = 23.096 0.4226x =
23.096 x = 54.64 m Therefore: h = x = 54.64 m
Answer: Height of tower = 54.64 m
Example 5: A man standing 80 m from a tower observes the angle of elevation to the top to be 38°. Find: (a) The height of the tower (b) The distance from the man to the top of the tower (line of sight)
Diagram 47:

Solution:

(a) tan 38° = h/80 h = 80 × tan 38° h = 80 × 0.7813 h = 62.50 m
(b) cos 38° = 80/s s = 80/cos 38° s = 80/0.7880 s = 101.52 m
Answer: (a) Height = 62.50 m (b) Line of sight = 101.52 m
Example 6: From the top of a building 40 m high, the angle of depression to a car is 28°, and the angle of depression to another car beyond the first is 18°. Find the distance between the two cars.
Diagram 48:
Building •
40m	\	|

Car1 Car2
←d₁→←d₂→

Solution: For car 1: tan 28° = 40/d₁ d₁ = 40/tan 28° d₁ = 40/0.5317 d₁ = 75.24 m
For car 2: tan 18° = 40/d₂ d₂ = 40/tan 18° d₂ = 40/0.3249 d₂ = 123.11 m Distance between cars = d₂ - d₁ = 123.11 - 75.24 = 47.87 m
Answer: Distance between cars = 47.87 m
Example 7: A plane flying at an altitude of 5000 m observes a ship at an angle of depression of 22°. After flying directly towards the ship for 1 minute, the angle of depression becomes 48°.
Find the speed of the plane in km/h.

Ship

Solution: Initial distance: tan 22° = 5000/d₁ d₁ = 5000/tan 22° d₁ = 5000/0.4040 d₁ = 12,376 m Final distance: tan 48° = 5000/d₂ d₂ = 5000/tan 48° d₂ = 5000/1.1106 d₂ = 4,502 m
Distance traveled = d₁ - d₂ = 12,376 - 4,502 = 7,874 m in 1 minute Speed = 7,874 m/min = 7.874 km/min = 7.874 × 60 km/h = 472.44 km/h Answer: Speed = 472.44 km/h
Example 8: Two buildings are 30 m apart. From the top of the shorter building (20 m high), the angle of elevation to the top of the taller building is 35°. Find the height of the taller building.
Diagram 50:
Top of tall building
 	/| 35° / |
\ / |x
/	|
Short	•_/	|_• 20m	| |	| |
| |	| |
|_|	|_|
· 30m →

Solution: Let x = additional height above short building
tan 35° = x/30 x = 30 × tan 35° x = 30 × 0.7002 x = 21.01 m
Total height of tall building = 20 + 21.01 = 41.01 m
Answer: Height of tall building = 41.01 m
Class Activity:
Activity 1: Make a simple clinometer using a protractor, string, and weight. Go outside and measure the angle of elevation to the school building top.
Activity 2: Draw diagrams for these situations:
Looking up at a flagpole from 20 m away
Looking down from a helicopter to a car
Activity 3: Solve: From 100 m away, the angle of elevation to a tower is 28°. Find the tower height.
Evaluation Questions:
Define angle of elevation and draw a labeled diagram.

What is the relationship between angle of elevation from A to B and angle of depression from B to A? Explain why.
From 60 m from a tree, the angle of elevation to its top is 42°. Find the height of the tree.
From a cliff 80 m high, a boat is seen at an angle of depression of 35°. How far is the boat from the cliff base?
Draw a diagram showing a person looking up at a bird on a tree, labeling the angle of elevation, horizontal line, and line of sight.
Assignment:
From a point 75 m from the base of a tower, the angle of elevation to the top is 36°. Calculate: (a) The height of the tower (b) The length of the line of sight from the point to the top
From the top of a lighthouse 50 m high, the angles of depression to two ships on the same side are 30° and 45°. Find: (a) The distance of each ship from the lighthouse base (b) The distance between the two ships
A ladder 12 m long leans against a wall. If it makes an angle of 68° with the ground, find: (a) How high up the wall it reaches (b) How far the foot of the ladder is from the wall
From a point on level ground, the angle of elevation to the top of a hill is 18°. After walking 200 m directly towards the hill, the angle of elevation is 26°. Find the height of the hill.
An aircraft flying at 8000 m altitude observes a landing strip at an angle of depression of 12°. How far (horizontally) is the aircraft from the landing strip?

WEEK 6: FREQUENCY DISTRIBUTION
Learning Objectives:
By the end of the lesson, students should be able to:
Define frequency and frequency distribution
Construct frequency tables from raw data
Determine class intervals and class boundaries
Calculate cumulative frequencies
Interpret frequency distributions
Entry Behaviour:
Students can collect and organize data, understand tally marks, and perform basic counting and arithmetic operations.
Instructional Materials:
Whiteboard and markers
Data collection sheets
Calculator
Chart showing frequency table examples
Real data sets (test scores, ages, heights)
Colored markers for tally marks
Content:
Meaning of Frequency
Definition: Frequency is the number of times a particular value or event occurs in a data set.
Diagram 51: Understanding Frequency
Example: Test scores of 10 students

15, 12, 15, 18, 12,15, 20, 12, 18, 15
COUNT
OCCURRENCES:ScoreTallyFrequency12|||315||||418||220|1Total: 10

FREQUENCY tells us:
How many times each value appears
Which values are most/least common
Distribution pattern of data

Types of Data:
Diagram 52: Data Classification
DISCRETE DATA
Countable values
Specific numbers
Examples: Number of students (1, 2, 3...)
Number of goals (0, 1, 2...)
Shoe sizes (38, 39, 40...)

CONTINUOUS DATA
Measurable values
Any value in a range
Examples: Height (1.52m, 1.53m, 1.534m...) Weight (45.3kg, 45.31kg...)
Time (2.5s, 2.53s, 2.531s...)

DATA TYPE affects how we group it!

Construction of Frequency Tables
For Ungrouped Data (Discrete):
Diagram 53: Ungrouped Frequency Table
Example: Number of children in 20 families

2, 3, 4,2, 5, 3, 2, 4, 3,6,3, 2, 4,3, 5, 4, 3, 2, 4,3
STEP 1: List all different values
STEP 2: Count frequency of each

Number ofTallyFrequencyChildren2|||| |53|||| |||84|||| |55||26|1Total:	21(Check!)
Note: Total frequency = total observations

For Grouped Data (Continuous):
Diagram 54: Grouped Frequency Table
Example: Heights of 30 students (in cm) 155, 162, 148, 171, 159, 165, 152, 168...

When data has many different values, GROUP them into CLASS INTERVALS

Height (cm)TallyFrequency145-149||2150-154|||3155-159||||5160-164||||4165-169||||5170-174|||3Total: 30

CLASS INTERVAL = Range of values (e.g., 145-149 includes all heights
from 145cm up to but not including 150cm)

Key Terms:
Diagram 55: Frequency Table Components
┌─────────────────────────────────────┐
│ CLASS INTERVAL (or CLASS)	│
│ Range of values grouped together	│
│ Example: 20-29, 30-39	│
└─────────────────────────────────────┘

┌─────────────────────────────────────┐
│ CLASS WIDTH (or CLASS SIZE)	│
│ Size of each interval	│
│ Formula: Upper limit - Lower limit │
│ Example: 29-20 = 9 (for 20-29)	│
│ Or: 30-20 = 10 (for 20-<30)	│
└─────────────────────────────────────┘

┌─────────────────────────────────────┐
│ CLASS BOUNDARIES	│
│ True limits of each class	│
│ Example: 20-29 has boundaries	│
│	19.5-29.5	│
└─────────────────────────────────────┘

┌─────────────────────────────────────┐
│ CLASS MIDPOINT (CLASS MARK)	│
│ Middle value of class interval	│
│ Formula: (Lower + Upper)/2	│
│ Example: (20+29)/2 = 24.5	│
└─────────────────────────────────────┘

┌─────────────────────────────────────┐
│ FREQUENCY	│
│ Number of observations in class	│
│ Count using tally marks	│
└─────────────────────────────────────┘ Steps to Construct Grouped Frequency Table: Diagram 56: Construction Steps
STEP 1: Find RANGE
Range = Highest value - Lowest value
STEP 2: Decide NUMBER OF CLASSES
Usually 5-10 classes Too few → lose detail
Too many → defeats purpose of grouping

STEP 3: Calculate CLASS WIDTH
Class width = Range ÷ Number of classes Round up to convenient number

STEP 4: Create CLASS INTERVALS
Start from lowest value (or below) Add class width repeatedly

STEP 5: TALLY the data
Go through data, mark tally in appropriate class

STEP 6: COUNT frequencies Convert tallies to numbers

STEP 7: VERIFY
Sum of frequencies = Total observations

Cumulative Frequency
Definition: Cumulative frequency is the running total of frequencies, showing how many observations fall below the upper boundary of each class.
Diagram 57: Cumulative Frequency

ScoreFrequencyCumulative
Frequency0-93310-1953+5 = 820-2988+8 = 1630-39716+7 = 2340-49423+4 = 2750-59327+3 = 30

MEANING:Total= 303 students scored 0-9
8 students scored below 20

-16students scored below 30-30students scored below 60
LAST cumulative frequency = TOTAL number of observations
Types of Cumulative Frequency:
Diagram 58: "Less Than" and "More Than"
"LESS THAN" (Most common)
Shows: How many values are BELOW upper boundary

Example:
Class
Frequency
CumulativeFrequency(Less than)10-194420-2961030-3981840-49523
Read as:
10 students scored less than 30
18 students scored less than 40

"MORE THAN" (Less common)
Shows: How many values are ABOVE lower boundary

ClassFrequencyCumulative
Frequency (More than)10-1942320-2961930-3981340-4955
Calculated from bottom up!

Example 1: The ages of 20 people are: 25, 32, 28, 35, 42, 38, 29, 33, 27, 31, 34, 40, 26, 37, 30,
36, 28, 41, 33, 39

Solution:
Step 1: Range = 42 - 25 = 17
Step 2: Class intervals (width 5): 25-29, 30-34, 35-39, 40-44

Example 3: The marks scored by 30 students in a test are: 45, 67, 52, 78, 63, 55, 48, 71, 59, 68,
51, 76, 62, 58, 73, 49, 66, 54, 70, 61, 53, 75, 64, 57, 69, 50, 72, 60, 56, 74
Construct a grouped frequency distribution using 7 classes.
Solution:
Step 1: Range = 78 - 45 = 33
Step 2: Number of classes = 7
Step 3: Class width = 33 ÷ 7 = 4.71 ≈ 5
Step 4: Classes starting from 45: 45-49, 50-54, 55-59, 60-64, 65-69, 70-74, 75-79
Step 5: Complete table:

MarksTallyFrequencyCumulative Frequency45-4950-5455-5960-6465-6970-7475-79Total30Example 4: Find the class boundaries for the class interval 20-29.
Solution: Class boundaries are found by:
Subtracting 0.5 from lower limit
Adding 0.5 to upper limit
Lower boundary = 20 - 0.5 = 19.5 Upper boundary = 29 + 0.5 = 29.5
Class boundaries: 19.5 - 29.5
Example 5: Calculate the class midpoint for the class 35-44.
Solution: Class midpoint = (Lower limit + Upper limit) ÷ 2 = (35 + 44) ÷ 2 = 79 ÷ 2 = 39.5
Class midpoint = 39.5

Since frequency must be whole number, let's check if there's an error in the problem. Assuming integer values:
If y = 6: x = 12 Check: 8 + 12 + 15 + 6 = 41 (too high)
If y = 5: x = 10 Check: 8 + 10 + 15 + 5 = 38 (too low)
The problem needs adjustment. Assuming total is 38 or 41, or y should be different.

WEEK 8: GRAPHICAL REPRESENTATION OF FREQUENCY
Learning Objectives:
By the end of the lesson, students should be able to:
Draw cumulative frequency curves (ogives)
Estimate median, quartiles, and percentiles from ogives
Interpret cumulative frequency graphs
Apply ogives to real-life problems
Compare different data sets using ogives
Entry Behaviour:
Students can construct frequency tables, calculate cumulative frequencies, plot points on graphs, and understand coordinate systems.
Instructional Materials:
Graph papers
Ruler and pencil
Calculator
Colored pencils
Pre-drawn ogive examples
Whiteboard and markers
Content:
Cumulative Frequency Curve (Ogive)
Definition: An ogive (pronounced "oh-jive") is a graph that shows cumulative frequencies. It displays the running total of frequencies up to each class boundary.
Diagram 59: Understanding Ogive
OGIVE CHARACTERISTICS:

SHAPE: S-shaped curve (sigmoid)
AXES:
X-axis: Class boundaries (upper)
Y-axis: Cumulative frequency
POINTS: Plot at upper class boundaries
LINE: Smooth curve through points

EXAMPLE OGIVE:

Cumulative Frequency
50|•|•40|•|•30|•|•20|•|•	 0 10 20 30 40 50
Upper class boundaries

The curve shows how frequencies accumulate as values increase.
Types of Ogives:
Diagram 60: "Less Than" vs "More Than"
"LESS THAN" OGIVE (Most common)
Plots cumulative frequency at UPPER class boundaries
Curve rises from left to right
Shows: How many below each value

Cumulative Frequency
50|
|	_--
40|	_-
|	_-
30| _-

 	---
|		 Upper boundaries

"MORE THAN" OGIVE
Plots cumulative frequency at
LOWER class boundaries
Curve falls from left to right
Shows: How many above each value

Cumulative Frequency
50|---
|	---			 40|		--
|	-
30|	--
|		 Lower boundaries
Steps to Draw an Ogive
Diagram 61: Drawing Process
STEP 1: Prepare cumulative frequency table with upper class boundaries

Class	Frequency	Cumulative	Upper
Frequency	Boundary
10-19	4	4	19.5
20-29	8	12	29.5
30-39	10	22	39.5
40-49	6	28	49.5
50-59	2	30	59.5

STEP 2: Set up axes
X-axis: Upper class boundaries Y-axis: Cumulative frequency

STEP 3: Plot points
(19.5, 4), (29.5, 12), (39.5, 22)
(49.5, 28), (59.5, 30)

STEP 4: Add starting point
Before first class: (9.5, 0) STEP 5: Join points with smooth curve
NOT straight lines!

STEP 6: Label axes and title graph

Important Notes:
Diagram 62: Key Points
STARTING POINT:
Always start at (Lower boundary of first class, 0)

Example: If first class is 10-19 Start at (9.5, 0)

PLOTTING POINTS:
Plot at UPPER boundaries NOT at midpoints!

WRONG:	CORRECT:
•	•
/|\	/
/ | \	/
|	•
30	30
(midpoint) (29.5)
(boundary)

SMOOTH CURVE:
Draw smooth S-curve NOT zigzag lines!

WRONG:	CORRECT:
/\	 	---
/ \	_--
/	\	-

Reading Information from Ogive
Median (Middle Value): Diagram 63: Finding Median
MEDIAN = Value at middle position

STEPS:
Find n/2 on y-axis (n = total freq)
Draw horizontal line to curve
Drop vertical line to x-axis
Read value

Example: n = 40
n/2 = 20

MEDIAN ≈ 35

Quartiles:
Diagram 64: Finding Quartiles
QUARTILES divide data into 4 equal parts

Q₁ = Lower Quartile (25th percentile) Position: n/4

Q₂ = Median (50th percentile) Position: n/2

Q₃ = Upper Quartile (75th percentile) Position: 3n/4
Example: n = 40

INTERQUARTILE RANGE = Q₃ - Q₁

Percentiles:
Diagram 65: Finding Percentiles
PERCENTILES divide data into 100 parts

P_k = kth percentile Position: (k/100) × n

Common percentiles:
P₂₅ = Q₁ (25th percentile)
P₅₀ = Median (50th percentile) P₇₅ = Q₃ (75th percentile)
Example: Find P₃₀ when n = 50 Position = (30/100) × 50 = 15 Cumulative

Draw horizontal at 15, read value on x-axis
Interpreting Ogives
Information from Ogives:
Diagram 66: What Ogives Tell Us
MEDIAN
Central tendency measure 50% above, 50% below

QUARTILES
Q₁: 25% below
Q₃: 75% below

RANGE OF MIDDLE 50% Interquartile Range = Q₃ - Q₁ Shows spread of middle data

PERCENTAGES
"What % scored below 40?" Find cumulative freq at 40,
divide by total, multiply by 100

NUMBER OF OBSERVATIONS "How many scored above 35?"
Total - Cumulative freq at 35

SPECIFIC VALUES
"Below what mark did 30 students score?" Find cumulative freq = 30,
read corresponding value

Comparing Two Ogives:
Diagram 67: Comparative Analysis
When comparing two data sets:

Cumulative Frequency
50|	Set A
|	/
40|	/	Set B
|	//
30|	_//
| _-/ 20|_-/
|/		 Values

OBSERVATIONS:
Set A has higher median (curve is to the right)
Set B is more consistent (steeper curve = less spread)
Compare Q₁, Q₂, Q₃ for both sets

Applications of Ogives
Real-Life Uses:
Diagram 68: Practical Applications
EDUCATION
Determine pass marks
Identify top 10% students
Set grade boundaries

BUSINESS
Analyze sales data
Determine inventory levels
Assess customer patterns

HEALTH
Growth charts
Weight distributions
Blood pressure ranges

ECONOMICS
Income distributions
Poverty lines
Wealth percentiles

QUALITY CONTROL
Defect rates
Product specifications
Performance standards

Example 2: From the ogive in Example 1, estimate: (a) The lower quartile (Q₁) (b) The upper quartile (Q₃) (c) The interquartile range
Solution:
Q₁ position = 50/4 = 12.5 From graph at 12.5: Q₁ ≈ 22
Q₃ position = 3 × 50/4 = 37.5 From graph at 37.5: Q₃ ≈ 40
Interquartile range = Q₃ - Q₁ = 40 - 22 = 18 marks
Example 3: The cumulative frequency curve below shows the weights of 60 babies.
Cumulative Frequency
60|•|•50|•|•40|•|•30|•|•	 2.0 2.5 3.0 3.5 4.0 4.5
Weight (kg)
Find: (a) The median weight (b) How many babies weigh less than 3.2 kg (c) The 80th percentile
Solution:
Median position = 60/2 = 30 From graph at 30: Median ≈ 3.1 kg
From graph at 3.2 kg: Cumulative frequency ≈ 35 35 babies weigh less than 3.2 kg
(c) P₈₀ position = (80/100) × 60 = 48 From graph at 48: P₈₀ ≈ 3.8 kg
Example 4: The table shows the heights of 80 students. Construct an ogive and estimate how many students are taller than 165 cm.

Height (cm)Frequency150-1545155-15912160-16418165-16925170-17415175-1795Solution:
Step 1: Create cumulative frequency table:

HeightFrequencyCumulative FrequencyUpper Boundary150-15455154.5155-1591217159.5160-1641835164.5165-1692560169.5170-1741575174.5175-179580179.5Step 2: Draw ogive (plot points and join smoothly)
Step 3: Find cumulative frequency at 165 cm From graph at 165: Cumulative frequency ≈ 40 Students taller than 165 = Total - Cumulative at 165 = 80 - 40 = 40 students
Example 5: From an ogive, the following information is obtained:
Q₁ = 25
Median = 35
Q₃ = 45
Total frequency = 100
Calculate: (a) The interquartile range (b) The semi-interquartile range (c) The percentage of data between Q₁ and Q₃
Solution:
Interquartile range = Q₃ - Q₁ = 45 - 25 = 20
Semi-interquartile range = (Q₃ - Q₁)/2 = 20/2 = 10
Data between Q₁ and Q₃: Q₁ is at position 25 (25% mark) Q₃ is at position 75 (75% mark) Percentage = 75% - 25% = 50%
Example 6: An ogive shows that 20 students scored below 40 marks, and 45 students scored below 60 marks. If the total number of students is 80, how many students scored between 40 and 60 marks?
Solution: Students below 60 = 45 Students below 40 = 20
Students between 40 and 60 = 45 - 20 = 25 students
Example 7: The median from an ogive is 52, Q₁ = 40, and Q₃ = 65. Comment on the distribution of the data.
Solution: Distance from Q₁ to Median = 52 - 40 = 12 Distance from Median to Q₃ = 65 - 52 = 13
Since the distances are approximately equal (12 ≈ 13), the data is approximately symmetrically distributed around the median.
If Q₃ - Median > Median - Q₁: positively skewed If Q₃ - Median < Median - Q₁: negatively skewed
Example 8: From the cumulative frequency graph, estimate the number of students who scored:
(a) Less than 35 marks (cumulative frequency at 35 = 28) (b) More than 55 marks (cumulative frequency at 55 = 75, total = 100) (c) Between 35 and 55 marks
Solution:
Students scoring less than 35 = 28 students
Students scoring more than 55 = 100 - 75 = 25 students
Students between 35 and 55 = 75 - 28 = 47 students

Activity 2: Using the ogive from Activity 1, estimate the median and quartiles.
Activity 3: From a given ogive, practice reading different percentile values.
